# QUESTIONS
## Concerning the
# SOUL.

->Propounded<-
->By Dr. Balthasar Walter.<-
->And Answered,<-
->By Jacob Behmen.<-

->*Also known as the Teutonic Philosopher.* <note>original: "Aliàs Teutonicus Philosophus"</note><-

->And in his Answer to the first Question is the<-

## Turned EYE,
->OR,<-
## Philosophic GLOBE.

->(Which in itself contains all Mysteries)<-
->with an Exposition of it.<-

->*Written in the German Language*<-
->In the year 1620.<- <note>original: "ANNO. 1620."</note>

->LONDON,<-
->Printed by *Matthew Simmons*, in the year 1647.<-

<summary>This is the title page for a 1647 English edition of Jacob Behmen's answers to forty questions about the soul, originally posed by Balthasar Walter in 1620. It introduces Behmen's concept of the "Philosophic Globe" or "Turned Eye," which he claims explains all spiritual mysteries.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Behmen, Jacob Boehme, Balthasar Walter, soul, Turned Eye, Philosophic Globe, Teutonic Philosopher, mysticism, 17th-century printing</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the introduction to the "40 Questions," establishing the historical context of the correspondence between the author (Jacob Boehme) and Dr. Balthasar Walter.</meta>

# A CATALOGUE
## OF THE
# 40 QUESTIONS

READER,

<image-desc>The letter T is rendered as a decorative woodcut drop cap with floral scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>The Author wrote this answer to these questions primarily for the sake of his friend who sent them to him, as well as for the benefit of all those who love the knowledge of mysteries. This friend was *Dr. Balthasar Walter*, who traveled in search of learning and hidden wisdom <term>hidden wisdom: often used to refer to "Sophia" or esoteric spiritual knowledge</term>. Upon his return home, he happened to hear of this Author in the city of *Görlitz* <note>original: "Gerlitz"</note>. When he had obtained an acquaintance with him, he rejoiced that at last he had found at home, in a poor cottage, that which he had traveled so far for without receiving satisfaction. 

Then he went to the various universities in *Germany* and collected such questions concerning the soul as were thought and considered impossible to be resolved in a fundamental and convincing way. He compiled this catalogue of those questions and sent it to this Author, from whom he received these answers according to his desire. In these answers, he and many others who saw them received full satisfaction.

---

### QUESTION 1

1. *From where did the soul proceed at the beginning?*
2. *What is its essence, substance, nature, and property?*
3. *How is it created as the image of God?*

<meta>catchword: 4. What</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the "40 Questions" as a collection of inquiries about the soul compiled by Dr. Balthasar Walter and answered by the author. It highlights the transition from academic uncertainty to spiritual "satisfaction" and presents the first question regarding the soul's origin and essence.</summary>

<keywords>Jacob Boehme, Balthasar Walter, Görlitz, soul, essence of the soul, image of God, mysticism, 17th-century philosophy</keywords>

17. *From where and why is there conflict <note>original: "contrariety"</note> between the flesh and the Spirit?*

20. *How does it return to God again?*
21. *Where does it go when it departs from the body, whether it is saved or not saved?*
22. *What does every departed soul do? Does it rejoice until the Day of the Last Judgment?*
23. *Do the souls of the wicked—without distinction—for so long a time before the Day of [Judgment]...*

# TO THE READER.

If we knew the preciousness of our own souls, we would confess and acknowledge with an inward feeling the answer to Christ’s question, when he said: *What shall it profit a man to gain the whole world and lose his own soul? Or what shall a man give in exchange for his soul?* The soul is so precious that nothing can truly be valued at so high a price. To save it is the greatest gain; to lose it is the greatest loss. Therefore, who will not highly prize the study and understanding of the way to save it?

Christ says, *He that will save his soul shall lose it, and he that will lose his soul shall save it.* But who understands this? We know it is the earnest desire of every soul to be saved, and to be happy and glorious, but the way is very unknown to us poor fallen souls. We can hardly imagine that losing ourselves will be the saving of ourselves. Christ also taught that the way to glory was through many tribulations <note>original: "tribulations"; meaning great sufferings or trials</note> and death. This is the way he entered into glory, and so have all the blessed from the beginning of the world, and no one can enter otherwise until the end of time. 

But how shall a soul know the way to lose and deny itself, so that it may assuredly attain eternal salvation? Let it listen in its heart and conscience inwardly to that Teacher whom it shall find there, who is in God himself. We have the testimony of Moses for this, who told the Israelites: "The word, the commandment, is near you, in your heart and in your mouth" (not the outward...

<summary>The author exhorts the reader to recognize the supreme value of the soul and explains that salvation is found through self-denial and listening to the internal divine Teacher.</summary>
<keywords>soul, salvation, self-denial, conscience, Christ, Moses, spiritual guidance</keywords>

# To the Reader.

but the *inward heart and mouth*). As also the *Apostle Paul* says to the *Romans*, that *Christ*—the *eternal, essential* word of *God*, the word of *faith* which they, the *Apostles*, preached—is near us in our hearts and in our mouths. In another place he says, *Do you not know that Christ is in you, unless you are beyond reproach?* <note>original: ἀδόκιμοι (adokimoi); meaning rejected, disqualified, or failing the test</note>. Furthermore, the *Apostle John* says that *God is love*, and he who loves; *God dwells in him, and he in God*. We all perceive this is true, for *in him we live and move and have our being*. This might be known even if the *Apostle Paul* had not said as much, for one of the *poets* of old spoke what he knew and said, *we are all of his offspring*, as the *Apostle* mentions it. 

Nay, we all know that he who does well is the servant of *God*, but he who does evil is the servant of the *Devil*, who rules in his heart. And though there is no one who does good—no, not one—nor can do so of himself while he is in this mortal life, yet through *Christ* in him, he can desire to do well. He can be sorry when he has been drawn away to do evil by the lusts of his own heart, by which the *Devil* tempts us to do evil. But if we will resist the *Devil*, he will flee from us. If we will stop doing evil and instead desire, try, and learn to do well, without doubt we shall be able through *God* who dwells in us. And then he will teach us all things and lead us into all truth by his *Spirit*. All this we shall fully understand, along with all *mysteries*, when *God* shall manifest himself in us—if we earnestly desire it with all humility, self-denial, the losing of our souls, and being nothing in ourselves

# To the Reader.

Out of his inward mystery <note>original: "Mystery"; referring to the hidden or divine revelation within the author's spirit</note>, he has manifested many things in this and other writings of his. Knowing these things will be exceedingly useful to the furtherance of the salvation of every soul. When I had read these, I was very much satisfied in my own soul, and I desire that others may be made partakers of them as far as lies within my power. I have therefore taken it in hand to put this treatise into English. I chose to do this from the original <note>likely referring to the original German text</note> rather than from any translations, because translations many times fall short of the author's own meaning, and because I found many errors in some of them. He is so deep in his writings that we have need to desire that our souls may be put into such a condition as his was in; otherwise, they cannot be fully understood. But the same God who satisfied his desires will satisfy ours, if we cast ourselves upon Him in our souls and let Him do with us what He pleases.*

Concerning the author, he was from his youth *much addicted to the hearing of sermons. Hearing that speech of our Savior's in Luke 11:13, "Your heavenly Father will give the Holy Spirit to them that ask it," and being troubled by the differences and controversies in religion (which he knew not how to satisfy himself in), he was so stirred up and moved to ask, seek, and knock, that he might know the truth. Upon which, by the Divine*

# To the Reader.

Drawing and will, *he was wrapped in Spirit into the holy Sabbath, in which he remained seven whole days in the highest joy by his own confession*. After he came to himself, he laid aside the folly of youth and was driven by Divine zeal earnestly to rebuke *impudent, scandalous, and blasphemous speeches*. He refrained in all his actions from even the least appearance of evil and continued to earn his living by the labor of his hands until the beginning of the sixth century <note>original: "sixt Seculum"; here referring to the turn of the century cycle</note>, which was the year 1600. 

At that time, he was possessed a second time by a divine Light. By the sight of a sudden object, he was brought to the *inward ground or Center of the hidden nature*. Yet, feeling somewhat doubtful, he went out into an open field, and there he beheld the miraculous works of the Creator in the signatures <note>The "signature" of a thing was a contemporary belief that the external shape or character of a plant or object revealed its inner spiritual essence or medicinal use.</note>, figures, or shapes of all created things, very clearly and manifestly laid open. Whereupon he was taken with exceeding joy, yet held his peace in silence, praising God. 

But ten years later, in the year 1610, through the overshadowing of the Holy Spirit, he was a third time touched by God and renewed. He became so enlightened that, lest so great a grace bestowed upon him should slip out of his memory and he should resist his God, he wrote privately for himself (without the help of any books but the Holy Scriptures) many books, the titles of which follow at the end of this work. These books contain such high and deep-grounded mysteries of the Deity as have not been revealed since the Apostles' times.

On the 18th of November, New Style <note>original: "Stilo novo"; referring to the Gregorian calendar</note>, early in the morning, he called one of his sons and asked if he also heard

Being answered that it had struck two, he said, "It is not yet my time; my time is three hours from now." In the meantime, he was heard to speak these words:*

> O you strong God of hosts <note>original: "Sabaoth," a Hebrew title for God as leader of heavenly armies</note>, deliver me according to your will. O you crucified Lord Jesus, have mercy upon me, and receive me into your Kingdom.

*When it was nearly six o'clock, he took his leave of his wife and sons and blessed them, saying, "Now I go from here into Paradise." Then he asked his son to turn him over, and sighing deeply, he mildly and quietly departed this world.*

If it were not for the great benefit that I believe may come to everyone who studies this, I would be sorry to be the instrument making such things known in my native language. I would be even more unwilling for it to be published for the view of so many different types of minds as have now emerged. But my hope is that goodness will get the upper hand, and that the fruits of the Spirit will prevail in subduing the lusts of the flesh. Now, I readily submit to the judgment of those who have a good desire—and of those who are not proudly and perversely wedded to their own private opinions (once they have considered this author's meaning)—as to whether this will not be as beneficial to us as it has been to other nations. Indeed, my mind is led to think that our troubled, doubting souls may receive much comfort leading to that inward...

# To the Reader.

...inward peace which *passes all understanding*: that all the disturbing sects and heresies arising from the darkness and malice of men and devils will be made to vanish and cease by that understanding which may be kindled in them from it. Those who rule will perceive how to effect all their good purposes, to the joy and happiness of those who are subjected to their government; and subjects will soon learn to obey in everything as the primitive martyrs did, even if they should live under such governors as the martyrs did. So God shall be glorified by all men’s love for one another, and peace will flourish over all the earth.

It may be some will think it so hard to attain the understanding of this author when they read the answer to the first question (which is far more difficult than any of the others because it contains the sum of them all in brief) that they will refrain from taking as much pains as they suppose is required. But if it should prove a little harder than other writings, the profit will compensate the labor with a hundredfold advantage. Yet let everyone read it themselves, or hear it read with their own ears, so that the misreporting of others does not hinder them from so great a benefit. They shall no doubt attain it, for I am convinced by my own experience that everyone may receive according to their vast or narrow capacity; I have been satisfied according to my own measure, though I be

# THE
## AUTHOR’S PREFACE
### TO
## BALTHASAR WALTER.

<margin>1</margin>
Dearly beloved friend, it is impossible for human Reason to answer these questions of yours, for they contain the chiefest and greatest mysteries, which are known to God alone.

<margin>
a Daniel 2:27.
And such an answer Joseph gave to the King of Egypt.
</margin>
2. Hence, Daniel says to King Nebuchadnezzar: *That which the King asks and desires of the learned men, Chaldeans, astrologers, and wise men, is not in their power. The God of Heaven alone can reveal secret things. It is not within my own Reason to answer the King; yet, so that the King may perceive the thoughts of his heart, God has revealed it—not because my Reason is greater than any other living man's.*

3. So likewise I say to you: *You shall be answered—not because my reason is greater than any man's living, but only so that you may perceive the thoughts, the earnest seeking, and the desire of your heart; it is given to me to answer you.*

4. *And you should not seek after these things so anxiously, for they are found in no outward reason. But to the Spirit of God, nothing is impossible. Since we are the children of God, and in Christ are born anew of God, the son sees very well what the father does in his house, and he also learns his father's craft and work.*

5. Seeing also that we ourselves are the mystery of God, we ought not to suppose that we must not meddle with such mysteries (as the Antichrist <note>The author likely refers here to institutional religious authorities who discouraged laypeople from seeking direct mystical knowledge.</note> teaches). For no one takes any of God’s mysteries for himself unless it is given to him; and St. James says, <sup>b</sup> *Every good and perfect gift comes down from above, from the Father of lights, with whom there is no change nor alteration.*
<margin>b James 1:17.</margin>

<summary>The author addresses Balthasar Walter, explaining that while human reason cannot grasp divine mysteries, God reveals them to the faithful through the Holy Spirit. He defends the right of the "newborn" Christian to seek deep spiritual knowledge against those who would forbid it.</summary>

<keywords>Balthasar Walter, divine mysteries, Reason vs. Revelation, Daniel, Nebuchadnezzar, spiritual rebirth, Antichrist, St. James</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface, establishing the theological framework for the work and directing the reader to his previous writings for a deeper understanding of creation.</meta>

<header>The Preface.</header>

<margin>
c *The Threefold Life.*
\* Note that by the words "divine essence," "substance," or "essentiality" in the writings of this author, he does not mean that essence which is God himself. Instead, he means whatever exists besides God which takes its origin from God—just as the eternal Ideas in the wisdom of God do. That which is essential according to the form or figure of the Idea, and that in which all Ideas lie, he calls the *Great Mystery* <note>original: Mysterium magnum</note> and the *Heavenly Secret* <note>original: Coeleste Arcanum</note>.
</margin>

6. *And seeing you seek so eagerly after such things, you become thereby even the cause of finding them. For God gives his mysteries both through worldly means and also without means; but so that no man might boast, he often makes use of very humble people to reveal them, so it might be acknowledged that they come from his hand.*

7. *You shall be answered with a very sound and deep answer, yet briefly summarized—not according to outward human reason, but according to the spirit of knowledge.*

8. *And although I could sufficiently show and demonstrate these things in a longer description, yet seeing they are all described and explained at length in my other writings, at present I set them down only briefly for the ease and delight of the reader, and so that it may serve as a short memorial of the great mysteries.*

9. *But he that desires to know these things fully and fundamentally, let him seek them in my former writings, especially in the* c *third part <note>referring to his book "The Threefold Life of Man"</note>. There he has the whole ground of the* \* *Divine Essence, and also of the creation of all things—of that which is eternal and of that which is corruptible. He explains how everything was made, how it came to be as it is, why it acts as it does, and what it shall be in the end.*

10. *And therein also lies the Key of the Great Mystery <note>original: Mysterium magnum</note>, as far as a creature is able to comprehend or endure it. We refer you there for further explanation; and so I commend myself to you in the brotherly love of Christ, in the year 1620.*

### ->Jacob Boehme<-

# The first Question Answered.

### QUESTION the First.

->*Where did the Soul originally come from at the beginning of the world?*<-

---

#### 1.

We have, both in our second and third book, sufficiently revealed the mysteries of the Soul according to the three Principles of the Divine Essence. In those books, we have also described through many details the eternal Center of eternal Nature, and also the Trinity <note>original: "Ternary"</note> of the Divine Essence. Moreover, we have explained what Eternity has always been, and how the beginning of the Creation <margin>or came to be.</margin> was brought forth, and what an Angel and a Soul are. Also, we have revealed the heavy fall of Lucifer, and furthermore, both the Mothers who have <margin>Engendering.</margin> brought it forth—the one producing <note>original: "procreating"</note> the heavenly Essentiality, and the other the Hellish—where we have also written of Light and Darkness.

2. Therefore, the reader will not understand this treatise very well unless he has read and well <margin>or understood.</margin> comprehended <margin>or book.</margin> the third part of our writings.

3. Although that understanding is not within human power alone, the way to it is very faithfully shown to him. If he longs to attain it, he shall obtain a <margin>Leader, or the Holy Ghost.</margin> Guide and director (if he follows our counsel) who will show him the key of the *Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium magnum"</note>, leading to that precious *Philosopher's Stone* <note>A legendary substance in alchemy signifying spiritual perfection and enlightenment.</note>, and to all *Mysteries*. Let no one think this is impossible, for with God all things are possible. He who finds God finds all things with and in Him.

4. Now you know <margin>In or by.</margin> by Reason that all things are originally sprung and derived from Eternity. The Holy Scripture also tells you this: in God are all things; "In him we live and <margin>Acts 17:28</margin> move and have our being," and we are his offspring.

5. And although men cannot say of God that the pure Deity is Nature itself, but rather that it is the Majesty within the Trinity, yet we must say that God is in Nature. Even so, Nature can as little reach or comprehend Him as the air can comprehend the sunshine. However, we must say that Nature is born in His will, and that it is a SEEKING, produced out of Eternity; for where there is no Will, there is also no Desire.

<summary>The author introduces an inquiry into the origin of the soul, referencing his previous works on the Divine Essence and the nature of God. He asserts that while the soul's origin is a divine mystery, it can be understood through the guidance of the Holy Spirit and the recognition that all things exist within God.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Divine Essence, Trinity, Creation, Lucifer, Great Mystery, Philosopher's Stone, Nature, Will of God</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's metaphysical explanation of the divine nature, specifically addressing the origins of the Trinity and the relationship between Light and Darkness within the eternal being.</meta>

# The first Question Answered.

6. But in God there is an eternal Will (which is himself) to beget his <margin>f Glance or Luster or brightness of his Glory.</margin> Heart or Son. This Will creates the <margin>g Or rousing.</margin> stirring or proceeding out of the will of the Heart, which is a Spirit. Thus, Eternity consists of <margin>h 1. Will; Father. 2. Heart; Son. 3. Proceeding or Efflux; Holy Ghost.</margin> three Eternal Forms, which are commonly called Persons—as we have very accurately <margin>i Declared or Expounded.</margin> explained in our third book.

7. Since we discern and know that there is not only Light and Majesty, but also Darkness, as is evident, it is necessary for us to know where that Darkness comes from.

8. In the Eternity beyond Nature, there can be no Darkness, for there is nothing there to produce it. We must look only into the Will and the Desiring, for a desire is an act of attraction.

9. And since in Eternity the Will has nothing but itself, it draws itself into the Will and fills the Will; this is its darkness. Otherwise, if it were not desiring, it would be a Nothing—<margin>k Or namely.</margin> an eternal stillness without <margin>l Or Being.</margin> Essence <note>original: "Essence"; here meaning substantial existence or being.</note>.

10. Thus, this Attraction creates <margin>m Moveableness.</margin> Mobility and Essences, which otherwise could not exist in the stillness. This also creates <margin>n Astringency or bitingness.</margin> harshness, hardness, and drought <note>original: "drouth"; meaning dryness or lack of spiritual moisture.</note>, along with <margin>o Acidness.</margin> sharpness.

11. Despite this, we cannot say that the Darkness swallows up the Light (which is the Eternal Liberty); for that which is Eternal cannot be altered or changed. Yet, we must say that Light and Darkness exist within one another.

12. Now, the Light is good and has <margin>p Or power.</margin> virtue, but the Darkness has harshness, hardness, and coldness. The desire of the Will creates Essences and attraction, which is a <margin>q Wriggling.</margin> stirring in the hardness. If that which is attracted stirs because of the drawing, it causes a jarring, by which Light and Darkness are mingled together in the sharpness.

13. We must consider how the free Light is sharpened within the Essence during that sharp stirring. Through this, we come to understand the <margin>r Lightning.</margin> fire-flash and the eagerness; yet we cannot say there is any tearing or rending.

14. For that which is Eternal and without beginning allows no severing, but stands like a wheel that begets itself within itself. You have a likeness of this in the Mind of Man: there is indeed a Will of rising and running, but no physical removing. The greater the Will is, the greater also is the <margin>s Or Being.</margin> Essence, and the more strongly it is sharpened.

15. Thus the still Liberty—which is originally neither Darkness nor Light—is sharpened in the sharp, attracting Desire, so that it appears as a flash that shines.

<summary>The author explains the Trinity as a process of the Eternal Will and describes how the "Desire" within the divine creates a tension between Light and Darkness, resulting in the "fire-flash" of existence.</summary>

<keywords>Trinity, Eternal Will, Father Son and Holy Ghost, origin of darkness, divine essence, attraction, fire-flash, spiritual mobility</keywords>

# The first Question Answered.

16. Also, we cannot say that the Liberty <note>original: "Eternall Liberty," referring to the state of God before or beyond manifestation</note> takes in the flash <note>original: "the flash," likely referring to the sudden ignition of divine light or consciousness</note>, for from Eternity it has had nothing: but we can certainly say that the Light and Splendor shine in the Liberty.

<margin>Or astringency.</margin>
17. For that which is free lets in the Light, but that which is not free—such as the harshness <note>original: "harshnesse," a quality of contraction or tension</焚> which creates Darkness and is material, to speak in a spiritual sense—does not receive the Light.

<margin>Mild or fluid.</margin>
18. This we can truly say: that whatever is transparent and mild, or not of a gross <note>original: "grosse," meaning dense or heavy</note> nature, takes in the Light. This is seen in water, which takes in the Light, while the harsh Earth does not.

19. Moreover, in Fire you have a sufficient manifestation of the Essence of all Essences. For you see that the Fire burns in a harsh, dry matter; it is the harsh Desire which enters into itself like a great anguish and reaches after the Liberty. There, it also receives the Liberty like a flash, and it is kindled by that flash so that it burns.

20. And although it must be understood that there is no such fire in the Eternal Essence as that which appears externally, yet it exists internally in the harsh Desire, while externally it remains dark. Therefore, the Eternal Fire is externally dark; but internally—as it exists in itself within the Will of the Eternal Liberty—it is a Light which shines in the Still Eternity.

<margin>Or different kinds or manners.</margin>
21. Now then, we understand that in Fire there are ten forms; all of these are born in the Will, and all belong properly to the Eternal Will. Therefore, we rightly say that the Eternal Will is God's Will, and that the Liberty which possesses the Will is God himself; for it is the Eternity and nothing else.

### The first Form.

<margin>Or longing.</margin>
22. First, there is the Eternal Liberty, which possesses the Will and is itself the Will. Now every Will has a seeking <note>original: "seeking," a fundamental drive or longing</note> to do or to desire something; and in this, it beholds itself and sees in the Eternity what it itself is. It makes for itself a mirror of its own likeness, for it sees what it itself is; and so, finding nothing but itself, it desires itself.

### The second Form.

23. The second Form is the Desiring, and yet it has nothing but itself. Thereupon, its desire seeks a model of its own Will within itself and makes itself pregnant, so that a darkness or overshadowing comes to be in the Will. The Will itself would not have this, but the Desire does; the seeking causes it, and yet there is nothing that is able to consume or expel the Desire.

<summary>The author explains the metaphysical transition from "Eternal Liberty" to the manifestation of Light and Fire, describing how the divine Will generates its own desire and forms.</summary>
<keywords>Eternal Liberty, God's Will, Fire, ten forms, harsh Desire, Still Eternity, Essence of all Essences, divine manifestation</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into the nature of the divine origin and the "Nothing" that precedes all created things.</meta>

24. For that which exists before the Desire, beyond all seeking, is Free and a "Nothing," and yet it truly is. If it were a thing that could be perceived, it would be an Essence <note>original: "Essence"; here meaning a tangible substance or defined being</note>, and it would have to exist within the substance that produced it. But seeing as it is without substance, it is Eternity itself—namely, the Good. For it has no source and is not subject to change; rather, it is Rest and an Eternal Peace.

25. But because this immense Space is bottomless, having neither number, end, nor beginning, it is like a Mirror. It is All things and yet acts as a Nothing. It beholds itself and yet finds nothing but an **A**, <margin>In the original text: Eye.</margin> which is its Eye.

26. **AU** <note>Boehme uses these letters as mystical symbols for the breath and the opening of the divine will</note> is the Eternal Origin of something, for it is the Eternal beginning and the Eternal end. Thus, the Eternal Abyss looks into itself and finds itself.

27. The **A** is below and the **V** is above, and the **O** is the Eye. Although it is not a substance itself, it is the Origin of all substance. In this state, there is neither "below" nor "above," but its <margin>As the sight of the eye.</margin> Mirror in the **AU** is a vision.

28. But since there is no foundation or ground, its Mirror is an eye like this **O**; for God himself says in the *Revelation*, "I am Alpha and Omega, the beginning and the end, the first and the last." <note>original: "A and O"</note>

<margin>b Similitude, parable, or figure. c God's Character T.</margin> 29. Consider us according to this precious depth, for we do not speak here according to the laws of Nature in a physical form, but according to the Spirit above Nature in the Divine Character **T**.

30. The <image-desc>A circle with a dot in the center</image-desc> is the Eye of God, the Eye of Eternity. This creates and is a Mirror, and it is a round circle like a Globe, not like a mere ring; for we cannot describe it any other way. By this, we mean the Globe of Eternity, wherein lies the foundation of Heaven and Earth, of the Elements, and also of the Starry Sphere. <margin>d Or Wheel or Orb. e God's wonderful Eye.</margin>

31. For it is a Globe like an eye, and it is the Eye of God's Wonders, wherein everything was seen from Eternity, yet without substance—as if in a Mirror or an eye. For it is the Eye of the Abyss, concerning which we have no pen, tongue, nor speech to write or speak of; only the Spirit of Eternity leads the eye.

For there is no "finding" in the Abyss, because there is no place or limit, but only the mere Abyss. And when it does find itself in the Eye, yet it finds nothing but the Eye, which is the globe.

35. For the Eye makes a beginning and an end; and because there is nothing that can give it, it gives to itself. And it is from eternity to eternity, the Eternity itself; it touches nothing, for it is a "nothing" in itself.

36. Then if there be a Will, which is the Eye, and which keeps the Eye, that keeping is the Desire of the Eye. And so the Desire causes an attraction in the Eye, yet nothing is there but the Eye. The Desire only attracts itself in the Eye, and impregnates the Eye with that which is attracted, so that it becomes full; and yet also nothing is there but a darkening of the Free Eye. Yet the Eye is not dark, but the Desire in the Eye is impregnated within itself.

37. For the Will of the Eye is still, and the Desire of the Will makes itself full, and the Eye continues free in itself. For it is free in and from eternity, and this we call the Eternal Liberty in all our writings.

From this come the essences in the Eye and in the Will, and yet the Will may not allow itself to be pulled away.

39. For its specific right is to be still, and to hold the Eye in the Circle within the Globe; yet it cannot defend itself from the drawing and filling, for it has nothing with which it can defend itself except the desire.

40. And here arises the eternal enmity and the contrary will: the Will does not want to be dark, yet its Desire makes it dark. The Will would willingly allow the motion, because that is how it manifests itself, but the drawing in and darkening does not please it. In truth, the Will itself is not attracted or darkened, but the Desire within the Will impregnates itself <note>The Desire acts as a creative force that fills the Will with substance</note>.

41. Now, when the Desire is stuck fast in the darkness, there is a great anguish; for it is troubled, attracted, and also darkened. It brings anxiety upon itself within itself and desires liberty. By pulling so strongly at that liberty, it tries to draw itself into it, and so it makes itself more eager, rough, and hard; the darkness becomes like a horrible, consuming sharpness.

42. For it snatches the Liberty into itself; but it is so sharp that it appears in the Liberty as a flash of lightning, which consumes the darkness with its eagerness. This is why <margin>Deuteronomy 4:24</margin> God says, *I am a consuming fire.*

43. By this, understand how every matter <margin>matter <note>original: "Materia"</note></margin> consists in the power of the true Fire, and how the floor shall one day be purged. For this is the origin of the Fire, which has all power; it consumes whatever the Desiring has made, whether it <margin>The Center of Nature</margin> be stone or mineral, for it is the sharpness of the Eternal Liberty and creates the Center of Nature.

44. But so that you may search even deeper, know that the Fire <margin>1. 2. 3. 4.</margin> originally consists of three forms: first, in the Desire; second, in the matter of that which is attracted (the darkness), in which essentiality <note>the state of having a physical or spiritual essence</note> proceeds from the attraction; and thirdly, the source of anguish. The Fire itself makes the fourth form, which is the flash; for the Liberty causes that and is the kindler of the source of anguish.

45. For the Desire in the darkness wants nothing else but the Liberty, and the Liberty is a light without shining. It is like a very deep blue color mixed with green, so that one cannot tell what color it is, for all colors are within it. The Desire, in its eager anguish and sharpness, breaks the colors within itself and creates a horrible, consuming flash, and

### The Fourth Form.

46. Now the Liberty <note>original: "Liberty"; here meaning the unconditioned freedom of the divine stillness</note> in the Desire does not allow itself to be bound or captivated, but changes itself from the red flash into Light, into a glance of the Majesty; and it is an exulting, great joy in that Liberty.

47. For the Eye is made manifest in the Light, and the <margin>a Or Substantiality.</margin> Essentiality is made manifest in the Will. Then it is known what Light or Darkness is. Also, in this way, Eternity is known; and so God’s Holiness always arises in the wonders from Eternity. It has neither limit nor beginning, for it is an eternal beginning contained in nothing but the Wonders, which are its own Essence, where there is neither limit nor <margin>b Time or years.</margin> number.

48. And thus nothing is known in the still Eternity but the glance of the Majesty and the Spirit which is born in the Will; and the Majesty has the <margin>c Government.</margin> Dominion.

49. Beloved friend, understand the sense correctly: we do not mean that the Birth grasps the Liberty from the outside, but rather within itself in its Center. It grasps itself in itself and creates Majesty in itself. Yet there is no "enclosing" there; rather, it is as when life arises from Death or from Nothing, which <margin>d Life.</margin> dwells only in itself. This is called a <margin>e Principle.</margin> Principle <note>original: "Principium"</note>. And that in which it dwells is called Nature, which has seven Spirits and Forms, as can be seen in our second and third books.

50. Yet this Principle has but one Spirit, which is the life of that Principle; and it has but one will as well, which is the <margin>f Or satiating.</margin> fulfilling of Eternity with the glance of the Majesty.

51. For this Principle is the Power proceeding from the will of Eternity. The entrance, or the eternal beginning of the Power, is the Life and the Spirit of the power, which thrusts forth the essences of the "Begettresse" <note>The creative, birthing quality of nature</note> and opens the origin of the Majesty.

52. And the whole Eye (which has thus <margin>g Or formed, or figured.</margin> made itself a Glass <note>A mirror or reflecting surface</note> in the **Alpha** and **Omega**) is all things. It is Eternity, and within itself—in the Eye—it begets the Majesty, which is the Heart and power of the Eye. It also begets the Spirit, which proceeds from the Power in the Heart, even from the fiery, light-flaming essences.

53. Thus, understand the Holy Trinity <note>original: "Ternary"</note> in one Essence: the Father is the Eternity without Ground <note>original: "without Ground"; the Ungrund or the bottomless void</note>, which is Nothing and yet all things; and in the eye of his glance, he sees that he is—

And in the Spirit is the moving of the Power, and the multiplication without ground <note>The "ground" (German: *Grund*) refers to a physical foundation or limit; "without ground" means infinite or bottomless.</note> or number, wherein consists an eternal, bottomless multitude, and all in Power.

55. For that which has no ground has no number, nor is there any shutting up or comprehension within it; and that which <margin>k Or without *ab extra*.</margin> is within itself cannot be known <sup>k</sup> externally <note>original: *ab extra* (from the outside)</note>, but it may be felt by the Spirit. Thus the Internal drives out from itself and manifests itself in figures; otherwise, God could not be known.

56. Thus God is together one Spirit: and is from eternity in three beginnings and ends, and that only in himself. There is no place found in his sight, and he has nothing in himself that may be compared to him; also, there is nothing which can search and manifest anything further than his Spirit, which always manifests itself from eternity to eternity.

57. He is an eternal seeker and finder of himself in the great wonders; and that which he finds, he finds in the Power. He is the Opener of the Power; nothing is like him, neither does anything find him, except that which yields itself to be his own—that enters into him. In that thing which denies its own existence, the Spirit of God is all things; for it is one single Will in the eternal Nothing, and yet it is in all things just as God's Spirit itself is.

58. And this, my beloved friend, is the Highest Mystery: Therefore, if you would find it, seek it not in me but in yourself—though not in your Reason <note>The author distinguishes between "Reason" (human logic) and "Will" (spiritual alignment).</note> either, which must be as if dead. Your desire and will must be in God, and so God becomes the will and the deed in you. Also, the Spirit of God brings your will into himself, and then you may well see what God is, and what Spirit's child this hand is, and from what kind of Spirit it writes.

59. Furthermore, I brotherly exhort you that you do not seek with such eagerness; you will not reach the bottom of it with such searching, although you are known and beloved of God. Therefore, we give you this for a rule, yet externally I have no power to give it to you.

60. But follow my counsel: leave off your laborious searching in Reason and enter into the will of God, into God's Spirit, and cast outward Reason away; then your will is God's will, and God's Spirit will seek you within you.

61. And if he finds your will in him, then he manifests himself

62. And let no fear terrify you; nothing can take this away except your own imagination. Do not let that imagination enter into your will, and so you shall work the wonders of God in his Spirit. Acknowledge me as your brother in him, otherwise I shall be as one who is mute to you. I tell you this out of good will.

63. In this way, we will open your eyes so that you may be freed from your laborious searching.

64. For you have pursued this even into your old age, and as far as I understand, you have not yet found that deep Mystery in the Spirit. But since it is God's will that you should know it, and have it given to you as a reward for your great labor, take care that you receive it rightly. Furthermore, do not "cast pearls before swine" <note>original: "Pearle before swine"; a biblical reference to sharing sacred knowledge with those who will only despise or misuse it</note>, who are not worthy of it, nor will be worthy of it for all Eternity.

65. For that which shall be revealed to you here belongs to the Children of God; therefore, be faithful and employ it according to the Spirit, and not according to human reason.

66. For this knowledge is so sublime that it will not endure worldliness <note>original: "earthlinesse"</note> which proceeds from covetousness, pride, self-glory, and arrogance. Although you are not such a person, look well into whom you pour this oil <note>a metaphor for spiritual anointing or sharing wisdom</note>, for it is poison to many. Let others seek for themselves as you have done; but give the Children bread that they may eat, and praise Our Father who is in Heaven, for it is given to you for that purpose.

<margin>Or secret Mystery.</margin>
69. But now it is at hand, that the hidden thing should be found, not only by me, but also by many who will be faithful, and humble themselves to God, and seek in his Spirit and will. It will be found in the Eye of God only, and nowhere else; therefore let no one dive deeper in searching elsewhere, or he will find the Devil.

70. Seeing then that Eternity is thus—which is yet Nothing, and yet there is Light and Darkness, Life and Spirit, which are all things—there is a seeking (that is, a desire) in both to <margin>Or that can find anything.</margin> find itself, though there is Nothing that can be found but the Spirit.

71. Now, seeing it has nothing that it can find, and yet the Desire goes on eternally forward, therefore the Desire is a figure of the seeking will, the likeness of the Eye of God; it is as a glass <note>original: "glasse"; here meaning a mirror</note> of the Eternal Eye which is called God.

72. Now this is in two ways: one according to the Light, and the other according to the Darkness. For the seeking is in both, and yet there is no departing of the one from the other. The Light is in the internal, and the Darkness in the external; and yet that which is most internal is also most external, but the Light is the middle-most.

73. For it is in the Nothing; therefore it cannot be the most internal, for it has no place nor limit. It is its own finding, which the Darkness does not find; but the will in the Darkness, which desires the Light, goes out from the darkness and remains eternally in the Light.

74. Now the Desire of the Light presents a model like itself, wherein Eternity is manifest: that is, all whatsoever the Spirit, in the eternal power of God, finds in itself from eternity to eternity.

75. This model is not God the Eternity itself; for it takes its beginning in the Spirit, and it is the wonder of the Spirit <margin>The Model.</margin> which it seeks and finds from eternity. And it is in the Eye of God as a figure, and all the wonders of the Abyss <note>original: "Abysse"; the bottomless depth of divine mystery</note> of Eternity are therein, and are beheld in the Light of the Majesty, as one wonder in many endless wonders.

76. Also it is an Image of God, a Virgin full of purity and chastity, and no begetter <note>original: "begettresse"; one who procreates or produces offspring</note>, for the Holy Spirit only opens the wonders in the Power.

<margin>Discovers itself. <note>original: "Erblichet"</note></margin>
77. Yet this Virgin is the likeness of God, his Wisdom wherein the Spirit discerns itself, and always and in eternity opens the wonders therein: and the more is opened, the more is in it.

<margin>The Wisdom.</margin>
78. For she <note>Wisdom, often personified as Sophia</note> is without ground or number, and as immeasurable as the Eye of God himself. There is nothing like her, and nothing can be found that may be compared to her; for she is the only likeness of the Deity, and the Spirit of God is her essence within her.

79. She is a circle and a model which so opens our mind that we see her, and God in her; for our will is cast into her, and she is in our will. Therefore we speak of God and see him in her as if in our own property, according to the hidden nature of humanity; this sight <margin>Or Seeing.</margin> is exceedingly precious.

<margin>Of the way or condition of.</margin>
80. We must also speak somewhat of Darkness: In itself, it is an enclosing, though there is nothing that bars it up; rather, it shuts itself up and begets itself. It is its own enemy, for it creates its own source without ground or number; it has no giver that can bestow this except for the Darkness’s own form.

<margin>Or draws into itself.</margin>
<margin>Or astringent.</margin>
81. It arises from the first Desiring, when the Desiring contracts itself and impregnates itself, so that it becomes a stinging, bitter, harsh, hard, cold, and wrathful fire-spirit. For the Desire causes harshness by the attracting force in the will, yet the drawing is stinging and the suffering is bitter. The Will does not want this, and therefore it goes out from the stinging and enters into itself, creating a distinct Principle wherein the Majesty appears.

82. Thus, great anguish arises in the bitter suffering; and yet there is nothing there that can suffer, but it exists this way in itself, and it is its own life. If this were not so, the splendor <margin>Or glance.</margin> of the Majesty would not exist. The one is the cause of the other, for the flash is in the Darkness, and the Light with the Majesty is in the Liberty <note>The "Liberty" refers to the "Unground" or the void beyond nature where God dwells in stillness.</note>.

<margin>Or differentiation.</margin>
<margin>Or Substance.</margin>
83. And this alone is the separation: that the Liberty is a still Nothing which receives the Light into it and makes the Darkness material. Yet there is no comprehensible essence <note>original: "Essence"</note>, but rather a dark spirit and power—a filling of the Liberty within itself, which is within the desire and not outside of it; for outside of it is the Liberty.

<margin>The Great Mystery <note>original: "Mysterium magnum"</note>.</margin>
<margin>Or Life.</margin>

But the source is unsearchable until the Will sinks down into it; then it will be found and felt when the Will loses its light <margin>Or Life.</margin>. Herein lies the ground of True Faith; let this be told to you, you teachers in Babel <note>A reference to the "City of Confusion," used here to criticize established religious scholars who lack spiritual insight.</note>.

# The First Question Answered.

The impression of the darkness is called the Ground <note>original: "Ground"; here meaning the underlying foundation or basis of existence</note>, wherein the source is a cause of the life. For the wrathful flash is the awakening of the life, although it is nothing there but in itself. It is also a Desiring, and that desiring is a seeking; and yet it can find nothing but a glass <note>original: "glaſſe"; a mirror or reflection</note> and a likeness of the dark, wrathful source wherein Nothing is.

86. For it is a figure of the earnest, wrathful flash, and of <margin>stern</margin> the sharp and <gloss>stern</gloss> severe power which is God’s—according to which <margin>or zealous</margin> he calls himself a consuming fire and an angry, <gloss>zealous</gloss> God.

87. And this glass is also without Ground, without beginning, and without end, and yet has an eternal beginning and an eternal end. It is the only cause that the Abyss <note>original: "Abyſſe"; the bottomless void or primal chaos</note> is blue, dusky, and fiery. It is the cause of the stars and elements; for the firmament is a second glass proceeding from this.

88. As there is a threefold source in everything, and each is always the glass, begetter, and cause of the other—nothing excepted—all things exist according to the essence of the Trinity <note>original: "Ternarie"</note>.

89. Seeing then there is a glass in the Abyss in which the source beholds itself, so it is also a figure and image of the source. It stands before the source and does or brings forth nothing, but is a virgin of the source wherein the wrathfulness <margin>or discovers itself</margin> of the flash <gloss>discerns itself</gloss> infinitely without number; and it always opens its wonders therein by the bitter spirit of the <margin>or rising</margin> <gloss>stirring</gloss> essences.

90. This has its life in the flash, so that it flies more swiftly than a thought; and even the thoughts of the creatures exist and proceed within this, as do the spirits of all living creatures with their root—each life according to its Principle.

91. And in this spirit of the fire-flash consists the Great Almighty Life, for it is consuming; as the flash consumes the darkness, and as the fire consumes all things and yet remains a life in itself. Yet it is a hunger and thirst and <margin>substance</margin> must have <gloss>substance</gloss> <note>original: "Eſſentiality"</note>, or else it remains a dark, hungry fire: a will to devour and to enjoy nothing, a will to rage and sting and to find nothing but itself. From this, essentiality (namely, the water) and also <margin>or substantiality, or earth</margin> <gloss>sulfur</gloss> <note>original: "Sulphur"; in alchemy, representing the combustible or soul-principle</note> is begotten and begets itself from eternity to eternity.

92. And here, my beloved friend, seek the first root of the soul in the fire-life, and the second in the life of the light, in the Majesty; and so you shall find God’s image and likeness, and <margin>or hidden secrets</margin> the greatest <gloss>mysteries</gloss> of the Deity lying therein.

93. And although there is such an eye of wrathfulness, wherein...

95. But do not seek as *Babel*—that great city of confusion upon Earth—has sought. We do not blame her for anything except her negligence and carelessness, and for seeking her own glory and power. By those means, she has ensnared herself in the wrathful anger of God, which has for a long time subjected her under its wonders and drawn many souls into its source. Consider this.

96. In the third book of our writings, this is explained at length. That book is somewhat easier to understand than this one, but in this book is the deepest ground of eternity, as far as a spirit can conceive. It cannot contain more; it may well be described more extensively, but not more deeply. This is because it is understood within the abyss in both principles, for the soul arises in the abyss in both principles, and in the spiritual will, within eternity.

97. And yet, if the soul is not wary and circumspect, the Devil may easily ride in its chariot—that is, in its will. But if it is circumspect and casts itself into the will of the majesty of God, then the Holy Spirit rides in its will, and the soul becomes his chariot.

98. And in this, you may now excellently search the ground of Heaven and Hell, of angels and devils, of evil and good, of life and death, if you seek as we shall further direct you.

### ->The Sixth Form of Fire<-

99. Since two principles exist together in one essence (as no one with reason can argue against, for every life consists of both "venom" <note>original: "venome"; likely referring to the sharp, stinging quality of the fire-source</note> and light, each in its own principle, and as it possesses the source, so it also possesses its light), we must therefore search for what sustains the life so that it is not extinguished, and what drives forth its source so that it can endure forever.

100. This also has two distinctions: the Light-life has its own source and driving force, and the Fire-life also has its own source and driving force, each within itself. But the Fire-life is the cause of the Light-life, and the Light-life is the lord of the Fire-life; and herein lies the <term>Great Mystery</term>. <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note>

# The first Question Answered.

101. For if there were no fire, there would be no light, and also no spirit. And if there were no spirit to blow up the fire, it would be smothered and darkness would remain. The one would be nothing without the other; therefore, they both belong together, and yet they divide themselves from one another, but <margin>Or removing.</margin> without any fleeing—and yet, there is a fleeing of the spirit.

102. You may understand it by this: look upon the glowing fire. First, there is the matter from which it burns, namely, the harsh, attracted, bitter substance, which has a source of <term>anguish</term> <note>original: "Anguish source"; Boehme uses "anguish" to describe the tension or "contraction" within matter before it is transformed into light.</note> and is a <margin>Dark body.</margin> dark body, whether it be wood or any such thing.

103. Now, when it comes to be kindled, you see three principles. First, the wood in the darkness with the external <margin>Property and condition, or quality.</margin> source of this world, which also has its own life, or else it would not take fire.

104. Now, the fire has a wrathful, harsh, strong, bitter, desiring <margin>Quality.</margin> quality which begets thirst—a devouring and consuming. This great bitterness is its true spirit, an enrager and awakener, which has all the essences of life in it. It is the power of life and of the driving force; otherwise, there would be no burning.

105. That creates the great anguish-seeking after liberty. In the fire, it attains that liberty, for it consumes the darkness in its wrathfulness, as well as the matter of the fire from which it burns.

106. And by this, we know that one spirit divides itself into two principles—into two spirits—but not <margin>Inseparably. <note>original: "Inseparabiliter."</note></margin> separately. And yet, they flee from one another, and the one does not catch or apprehend the other, even though the one is the life and cause of the other.

107. Therefore, they are two principles, seeing they have a twofold source and life. And yet, there is but one root from which they proceed: one of them provides life, and the other provides food for that life. This is a wonder, and yet no wonder, for there is nothing that can wonder at it, because it is itself all things in one single essence.

<margin>
1. Fire.
2. Seeking.
3. Desiring attraction.
4. Substantiality or Corporality.
5. Sting.
6. Anguish.
7. Liberty.
</margin>
108. Now, the fire in itself is first a seeking to draw into itself, and that is the substantiality—the *plurality*—for the seeking creates it in the desiring by its attraction; otherwise, there would be nothing. This attraction is the bitter sting, a destroyer, which the substantiality cannot endure and will not suffer. That refusal to suffer is an anguish—a will to overcome the substantiality with the bitter sting. This anguish pierces into itself and reaches for liberty; and that liberty is a light in comparison to darkness.

109. Now the anguish is a horrible sharpness, and in this way the Liberty <note>original: "Liberty" (the divine freedom or the "Nothing" before creation)</note> is seized and sharpened until it becomes a flash of fire. The will of the anguish, in the sharpness of that bitter flash, consumes the substantiality <note>the material or essence of a thing</note>, whether it be wood or any other substance.

110. Now, when the fire has consumed the substance, the anguish becomes darkness again, and the flash remains hidden within itself once more. It is an extinguishing; the anguish remains in the darkness just as it was at first, before the fire flashed. It remains only as a terrible source of torment, where the bitterness is constantly made more dreadful by the harsh attraction <note>the "drawing in" or contraction of the dark desire</note>.

111. This process follows the Outward Principle <note>the physical laws or nature</note> of this world, as we see undeniably through experience. Since there is an everlasting essence in Eternity, we demonstrate it in this way: behold and consider it deeply, and read this with diligence.

<margin>The Liberty.</margin>
<margin>Understand by this the sinking of the hunger and thirst of Hell, and of Anger.</margin>
112. The sinking of the anguish into the eternal darkness is an eternal hunger, an eternal thirst, and an eternal desiring. Because the darkness in itself can reach nothing in Eternity that can satisfy it from out of the Liberty, it is rightly and truly the hunger and thirst of the abyss of Hell and of the anger of God.

113. But because the will in the anguish can reach or find nothing, it creates a figure and an image for itself within its own desiring through its eager attraction. This eager, harsh, bitter, dark essence is the material image itself; it eats itself and is itself the fuel for the fire, so that the eternal flash may always continue. Thus, the wrath is an eternally continuing burning; it burns eternally out of the darkness and has its own life within itself—namely, the bitter sting of the anguish, which rages and raves. This is the stirring <note>original: "Rigling," meaning a wriggling or restless movement</note> and the origin of life, and that is a Principle <note>original: "Principium," a fundamental source or beginning</note>.

114. Understand by this an eternal desiring and seeking, an eternal coveting, and yet having nothing but itself. It is an eternal envious enmity, a seeking of the essences <note>the fundamental qualities or "powers" of being</note>, where an innumerable and bottomless multitude is always born in the will. It is an eternal craftiness, a continual rising in the hunger, and an eternal finding of the image of its own desire—the image of the essences—within the will. This is made manifest in the flash, for the flash always elevates itself above the darkness, and the essences are in the flash and are continually brought into the will.

<margin>Sucking or attraction.</margin>
115. Thus the fire-will is a seeking <note>original: "sucking"</note> of high-swelling pride and a contempt for the darkness. It despises its own root; it is covetous and wants to devour more than it has, or more than it should. It possesses all lusts, for the desiring essences are made manifest in...

122. Now look closely, with very clear eyes: God created this world and every substance in six days, and they were finished about the middle of the sixth day, somewhat in the afternoon toward the evening. Then the Rest, and the Sabbath of the seventh day, began on that sixth day. And so, the Eternal Rest met the beginning of the Creation on the afternoon of the sixth day; this was the end, where the beginning and the end came together as one again, and it became manifest what God had made during those days.

123. Seeing then that man, by his imagination, has destroyed his heavenly angelic body and has brought it into a corruptible number—that is, into the outward principle <note>original: "outward Principle"; referring to the physical, material world governed by time and decay</note>—he now exists within it. Because he has lost the paradisiacal number and is placed in the "hundredth number," he is now surrendered to the outward life as his leader. That is, he has given himself up to this leader, so that the number of years he must fulfill in the cycle of this outward principle is clearly known to us.

<margin>i. Or Judgment Day.</margin>
124. If we knew for certain the exact hour of the sixth day when the Creation was finished, we could then determine the year and the day of the Last Day <note>The Day of Judgment</note>. For time does not go a minute further; its limit is hidden within the inward circle.

125. Therefore, know for certain that the time is near; for in the afternoon of the sixth day, the rest of the Eternal Day began. Therefore, God instituted the Sabbath of the seventh day as a rest and an everlasting remembrance.

<margin>k. Working—namely, the wonders.</margin>
126. And just as the Rest began on the sixth day toward the evening—marking the entrance to the manifestation of the works of the Creation (the end then joining the beginning again, so that the six days stood in a circle as a wonder)—so you must know that you were created in Paradise, yet you have departed from it into the spirit of wrathfulness and into death. This spirit <margin>l. Which spirit</margin> has now worked its wonders in you for these 5,500 years and more.

127. And now the end has found the beginning again. You shall see, feel, and find what Paradise once was—at least, every one of those who shall be born in God shall do so.

128. For (to speak according to human reason, and not strictly according to God's perspective), Paradise is born again. You shall not escape mortality, nor the wrath present in the flesh, but Paradise is now already manifest in the mind and in the soul of the children of God, and they have a true taste of its power.

129. No subtlety or power can hinder it, no cleverness can suppress it, nor can any devil destroy it; for the end has found the beginning, and there can be no obstacle to it. The power of falsehood is breaking, and nothing remains but to wait for the bridegroom <note>A biblical reference to the return of Christ</note>; for the children of God shall be found in Paradise.
<margin>x. The end</margin>

132. So also consider the Eternal Fire further, and take an analogy from all sorts of fire in this world; for that which is a Spirit in Eternity is a substance in this world. You do not see the source; you can only feel it.

Then, in that wrathfulness, Time began, and the Creation followed that manner which is mentioned in the Third Book.

135. Now you see also very well that fire must have fuel to burn, or else it is a darkness; and although it devours itself (by its eager attraction), yet that fire is nothing but a source...

<meta>This page continues the author's metaphysical explanation of the relationship between God's wrath (the dark fire) and God's love (the light), using the physical properties of fire and water as an analogy for spiritual truths.</meta>

# The First Question Answered.

...in the Darkness, which we understand to be the Abyss of the Anger of God. This anger is not manifested in God's own being, but exists only as a cause of the Life in the Kingdom of God.

<margin>Substance.</margin>
136. You see that all fire must have <term>matter</term> <note>original: "substance"</note>, or else it will not burn. Understand it this way: the fire produces Air, and in the Air, water; it mightily attracts the Air with the water back into itself, by which the intensity of the fire is so tempered that it shines.

<margin>Glowing.</margin>
137. For without water no fire shines. If no water can be produced in a thing, the fire in that thing will not shine, but only glimmer. For example, consider a red-hot stone, which has the heat of the fire but no brightness, only a glimmer—and hardly even that. But in Iron it shines, because in Iron the fire has water. Therefore, Iron eventually is consumed and develops Rust, but a stone does not. This is how it works according to the Outward Principle of this World; but according to the inward principle (namely, the Kingdom of God), it is as follows—observe it closely.

138. The Eternal fire burns Eternally; yet it is a Spirit. It is not manifested according to its wrathfulness in the Kingdom of God. Understand it thus: the flash creates a shining which arises from the fire, and yet it does not dwell in the wrath of the Fire. Instead, it completely satisfies the fire and sends light out from the fire. It is neither captured nor held back by the fire, but carries with it another quality of its own, namely <term>Meekness</term> <note>original: "Amiableness or Friendliness"</note>. Yet, it still possesses the Power, Wisdom, and <term>Skill</term> <note>original: "Art"</note> of the Fire, for in the Light, the <term>essences</term> <note>original: "Wit, reason, or knowledge"</note> of the Fire’s source are first revealed.

<margin>Amiableness or Friendliness.</margin>
<margin>Wit, reason, or knowledge.</margin>
<margin>Or pain.</margin>
139. Now the light creates no <term>suffering</term> <note>original: "source" or "pain"</note>, but it enters into itself into a state of Meekness. Yet it is still desiring, a desire which proceeds from the source of the Fire; its desiring is an attraction of Meekness and power into itself, and so it makes itself pregnant with Meekness.

140. For the Light is a Fire also—a very yearning fire, a desiring fire, and a perpetually finding Fire, which always finds what is generated in the Original source.

141. All the Power which arises in the wrathful fire is manifested in the Light, and the Light desires it in meekness. For the wrathfulness of the Fire and the shining of the Light are two Principles of a twofold source, each dwelling in itself. One does not comprehend or contain the other throughout Eternity, and yet the one is the life and the cause of the other.

<margin>Or pain.</margin>
142. And we must understand it thus: we must consider that a horrible, anguishing <term>source of pain</term> <note>original: "source"</note> creates a sinking into itself, like Death, where the boundary of Separation exists. Yet the Anguish keeps its source within itself; but the sinking into itself (as if into Death) enters into its <term>receptacle</term> <note>original: "Aether" or "Sky"</note>, wherein the life of the Anguish resides.

<margin>Sky, or receptacle.</margin>

<summary>The author uses the physical properties of fire, light, and water to explain the spiritual relationship between God's "wrathful fire" and His "light of meekness," arguing that while they are distinct principles, they are interdependent. He posits that light tempers the fire's rage, transforming it into a productive, living force.</summary>

no more known: for the sinking breaks forth from the source of anguish as a dying—and it is a dying—and yet in Eternity there is no dying, but a kind of entering into another world, of another principle, and of another source.

143. For the sinking enters into the still Eternity, namely into the Liberty <note>original: "the Liberty"; referring to the divine freedom or the void beyond nature</note>. And just as the source of the wrathful fire remained within itself in its own life, so the sinking is a going quite out from the fire-life. Yet it proceeds from the fire-life, but it does not possess its source of pain, for it is broken off from that in Death. The limit of this separation is a Death; so that the sinking Life pierces through Death and sprouts through Death into another world. It has another Substantiality (namely, another kind of water) wherein the Light shines, and therein is no wrathfulness.

<margin>The foundation or bottom.</margin>
144. For in Eternity there is no Death to detain anything, but there is a kind of entering into several conditions. For that which has no beginning also has no end and no ground <note>original: "ground"; the underlying basis or foundation of being</note>. And thus the Light arises from the source of the fire.

145. For the Light dwells in the Fire, and yet not in the Fire; it is another world, and it is another Fire, called Love, Power, Wonder, sweet, mild, and pure. It is no physical substance, nor is it Nature, but it is beyond Nature <note>original: "Extra without"</note> in another Principle.
<margin>Outside or without.</margin>

146. It is nothing but a Light-flaming, powerful Majesty, and it has its own Spirit. This Spirit brings the sinking through Death; and this sinking out of the anguish through Death creates the sprouting forth through Death.

<margin>The Spirit.</margin>
147. The Spirit is free in itself, both from the fire and from the Light, and it is not held or captivated by either of them (no more than the Fire detains the Air). It proceeds from the Light, from the power of the Light, and it opens all that exists either in the source of the Fire or in the source of the Light.

148. Yet it has no feeling of the Fire within it; rather, it is a blower-up of the fire of Light, a producer of the essences of Love in the desiring power, and an opener of the essences of Love.

149. And so that we might be understood when speaking of the essences of Love as another Fire, let it be observed: behold, when the light is brought forth through the wrathfulness—so that one fire goes forth through the other—then the fire of Light desires the wrathfulness no more. It is dead to the wrathfulness and is a distinct Fire in itself. It sends forth its life out of itself, which is a sprouting; for it is both desiring and attracting, from which essences proceed. It has all forms within it, just as the fire-life has, and also a similar rising.

<margin>Or Virtue.</margin>
150. And yet the essences are born out of the power <note>original: "vertue"</note> of the Light; and when they fully taste one another, there is a pure Desire.

<summary>Jacob Boehme describes the mystical transition from the "fire-life" of anguish and wrath into the "light-life" of love and divine liberty, explaining how life sprouts through death into a new principle. He defines the Spirit as a free agent that proceeds from the Light to open the essences of divine Love.</summary>

Thus, this Will becomes satisfied, and yet it is nothing but this Power, which is an Image of the Wonders. It is a likeness of the Birth, and it is the power itself; it is the Essence of the Spirit from which <margin>Moves or waves.</margin> the Spirit has its food. It goes forth from the Image and floats as the air in this world does.

151. Now, the Spirit finding nothing like <margin>outside of itself.</margin> itself, and so not finding itself except in the Power, it is therefore desiring. For it dwells in the Ground of the Power and yet is not the Power itself; therefore, its desiring creates a likeness of itself.

152. For a desiring is a seeking, and the figure of the seeking is within the seeking; the figure makes the seeking manifest. Thus, the Spirit also dwells in its own Figure, in the Power, and in the <margin>That is, the Desiring.</margin> Light of the Majesty; and it is an Image according to the property of the Spirit.

153. The Spirit is not the Image, but the seeking and its desiring is the Image; for it dwells in itself, in its seeking. In its figure, it is a different Person than the figure of the Power, and according to this Essence, God is said to be Threefold in Persons.

154. # The Exposition of the Philosophical Globe or Eye, concerning both the Threefold * Circles which signify especially the two Eternal Principles; the Third being also clearly understood therein, and how it must be understood.

<margin>The Third Principle.</margin>

It represents the Eye of the Essence of all Essences—the Eye of God—which is the Mirror <note>original: "Glasse"</note> of Wisdom, wherein all wonders have been seen from Eternity. Here it is described how that Eye has entered into a tangible being <note>original: "Essence"</note>, for the reader of this book to contemplate.

<margin>That Mind.</margin>
163. This is not to suggest it can be fully described or pictured, for the Mind alone apprehends it—and only that mind which can walk in the Divine Mystery. This is achieved not by Art or human Reason, but by that understanding which the Spirit of God opens to the human spirit of the soul within the Great Mystery; otherwise, it cannot be grasped.

### Concerning the half of the Triple Circle on the Left hand, and of number 2.

<margin>Outside of Nature.</margin>
168. The triple circle on the left hand (where there also stands outside the circle at Number 2 the Mystery <gloss>outside of Nature</gloss>) signifies how the Abyss <note>original: "Abysse"; the infinite, bottomless void before creation</note> brings itself into a Foundation. That is, how the Eye of Eternity—namely the First Will, which is called the Father of Eternity and of all beginnings—brings itself through Wisdom into the Trinity, into an Eternal Foundation, where it dwells within itself and possesses itself. It also shows how the Will brings itself into Nature, and how Essence, perceptibility, and perception arise.

### Concerning the half of the Triple Circle on the Right hand.

169. The other triple circle on the right hand signifies the Divine Essence of the Holy Trinity and the Angelic world. This world arises from the Great Mystery of Eternity and is made manifest by the Principle of Fire.

### What the Cross signifies.

<margin>Or semicircles, or half-globes.</margin>
170. The Cross, whose arms go through both the triple <gloss>semicircles</gloss>, signifies the Persons of the Deity. It shows how they distinguish themselves in the Eternal only-begotten state <note>original: "unigeniture"; the state of being the only-begotten Son</note>, as is mentioned further hereafter according to the Numbers.

### Concerning the Eye in the Circle.

<margin>Or two arms.</margin>
171. The Eye in the Circle, through which the Cross goes with an <gloss>angle</gloss>, represents two worlds in its two halves. The half on the left signifies the Great Mystery of the Dark world, where the Eye of Wonders brings itself into Nature. The half on the right signifies the Light world, where the Divine Mystery—having brought itself forth through the fire—dwells in the Majestic Light along with the first Mystery of the Wonders.

### Concerning the <image-desc>a small heart symbol</image-desc> in the angle of the <image-desc>a small Greek cross symbol</image-desc>

272. The Heart in the angle of the Cross signifies the Foundation or Center of the Deity. This does not mean it is a separate thing or occupies a specific physical place (for the Heart itself is the place or Foundation of the Deity and is the center everywhere). Rather, it is drawn this way so that people might learn to distinguish God from Nature, and so that Christians may learn to understand Regeneration—namely, how God has regenerated us in Christ, out of His Heart upon the Cross. Therefore, this figure is sketched in this way, so that

### Abyss: Number 16

<margin>Outside or beyond.</margin>
185. After "Image" stands "Abyss," number 16, signifying that the true Image stands in the Abyss <margin>Outside or beyond.</margin> beyond all source <note>original: "source," here meaning a point of origin or a specific elemental quality</note> and dwells in Nothing—that is, in itself only—and through it, God dwells. ### Of the word Omnipotence: Number 17; and Wrath: Number 18

<margin>Or boundaries.</margin>
186. Furthermore, at the left hand at number 17, there stands "Omnipotence," and it also stands outside the <margin>Or boundaries.</margin> circle of Nature. Therefore, the word "Wrath" stands in the space under the line at number 18, which signifies that the Wrath does not touch the angle of the <margin>Or the number Three.</margin> Trinity <note>original: "Ternarie," referring to the Holy Trinity</note>, but is born in the Desire.

### Craft: Number 19

<margin>Or Subtlety.</margin>
187. "Craft" stands at number 19, under the word "Omnipotence." This signifies the Essence coming out of the mirror of the Principle. This "Craft," in the second Principle, is changed into a right understanding; but here in the Magic fire, it is only craft, for it is subtle and sharp, and a cause of the understanding.

### Devil: Number 20

188. Opposite "Craft," "Devil" stands at number 20 in the space of the dark world. This signifies the malice of the Devil, in that he has departed from the point of the Trinity and has placed his will into Wrath and craft on purpose to domineer over the meekness of God, and to use the strength and power of the Fire and Wrath.

### Devil’s Art: Number 21, 22

# The first Question Answered.

Devils stand outside the Circle of Nature, and Art stands within the Circle of Nature. This signifies that the Devil was created out of the Mystery of the Father upon the line or stroke of the Cross in Eternal Nature, just as the other Angels were. But he obtained his "Art" (Number 22) through a magical <margin>p Or sucking, or attraction.</margin> seeking of Nature in the center of the dark world, whereas he should have obtained it in the power of the Heart of God; that is the cause of his fall and of his envy.

### Will; Number 23.

190. Over the line at number 23 stands "Will," signifying that the Devil has raised himself up from the Divine Line (upon which he was created) as a proud spirit, who desired to be *his own Lord, and to have ruled by his own art and wit.*

### Darkness; number 24.

191. This is also what the pride and subtlety of men do now: they raise themselves up from the Line of God and from obedience into their own "selfhood." In this state, the will cannot reach divine power and light, but instead falls into itself—into the dark, anguishing magical fire. As noted above the word "Will" with number 24, it falls first into Darkness; for Reason loses divine understanding and the divine desire through which it can receive the essence of God and so <margin>q Or fill.</margin> impregnate itself with <margin>r Or virtue.</margin> power from God.

### Fire; number 25.

<margin>s Or Reason.</margin>
192. And then Reason kindles the magical fire of covetousness <note>original: "Covetousnesse"; here meaning an insatiable, grasping desire for worldly things.</note>, so that it desires to have <margin>t Or more.</margin> much, and never has enough, as shown here at Number 25.

### Anguish; number 26.

193. And when it has filled itself with covetousness, then the magical fire begins to burn in Anguish (Number 26). For that which is thrown into the fire by covetousness serves as fuel for the magical fire in which the fire burns; and there Death is born, which must separate what covetousness has brought in.

### Death; number 27.

194. And herein also consists the grievous fall of *Adam*, who imagined as the Devil did, and desired to have the variety of this world as his own. He wanted to be cunning and obtain much <margin>* Or wit.</margin> skill, and even the earthly and hellish source within that skill. Had he continued upon the stroke in the Line of God, he would not have been earthly; for the spirit of his will would have dwelt in God and would have brought divine food into the body. But now he is in the Anguish (Number 26).

It argues that turning away from the Divine Line toward self-interest creates a "magical fire" of insatiable desire.</summary>
